  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I feel a bit embarrassed saying it, because it probably sounds vain or silly, but I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks—especially in lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or other staff are noticing it too instead of paying attention to what I’m teaching.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face, really. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but it’s that one side looking crooked or swollen in the lesson videos that I get stuck on. Once I notice it, I just feel like the students, parents, or staff must be looking at that instead of actually listening to me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to feel better about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, I suppose I do. I try to keep it reasonable, but before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll adjust the camera angle a lot, and replay little clips to check how my face looks.\n\nSometimes I’ll sort of cover part of my face with my hand while I’m checking, to see if it looks less noticeable. And… if it still looks wrong to me, I might re-record the whole thing. More times than I’d like to admit, honestly. It can end up taking so long that I miss prep work or I’m late getting to class, which I know isn’t great.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about your face cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… honestly more than I’d like to admit. I try to tell myself it’s not a big deal, because it’s just my face on a video, but once I see that crooked, kind of swollen-looking side, it really gets in my head.\n\nOn a normal day, if I’ve been recording or I know parents or staff might see a lesson, I can’t stop thinking that they’re staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying. It makes me feel really tense and embarrassed, and I know that probably sounds a bit ridiculous, but it can take over most of my focus.\n\nDoctor: Does the part of your face that you think looks crooked or swollen always look that way to you, or are there times when it looks normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… there are probably times when it looks normal, I think. I don’t want to say it’s constant every second. But when I’m watching those lesson videos, I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked or sort of swollen-looking.\n\nAnd once I notice it, it’s like that’s all I can see. I start thinking the students, or parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying. I know that may not be a very rational answer, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: When you've asked others, such as friends or family, if they notice anything unusual about your face, what do they say?\n\nPatient: They usually say they don’t really see what I’m talking about, or that it just looks like a normal camera angle thing. My family especially will say, “You look fine,” which… I’m sure they mean kindly.\n\nBut I don’t know, when I watch the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking. Once I notice it, I start feeling like the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me. I know that might sound excessive, but that’s honestly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does your perception of which side of your face looks crooked or swollen ever change, or is it always the same side?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same side, I think. At least that’s the side I keep noticing in the lesson videos.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, because maybe I’m just focusing on it too much, but once I see that crooked, swollen-looking side, I really can’t unsee it. Then I start worrying that the students, parents, or staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face ever pop into your head when you're not intentionally thinking about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I try not to sit around thinking about it, but it does sort of pop in, especially after I’ve seen myself on one of the lesson videos.\n\nI’ll be trying to focus on teaching or just getting on with things, and then I suddenly remember that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I start worrying that the students, or parents, or staff are noticing it too, instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit much… but that’s honestly what I’m struggling with.\n\nDoctor: When you've shown these videos to others, have they ever pointed out the flaw in your face that you're concerned about without you mentioning it first?\n\nPatient: Not really… no one has pointed it out first, if I’m being honest. Usually it’s me asking, or sort of trying to show them what I mean.\n\nBut I’m here because I can’t seem to stop seeing it in the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it, I just get this feeling that the students, parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying. I know that might sound like I’m making too much of it, but… that’s what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that maybe your face doesn't actually look crooked or swollen, and it's just how you're perceiving it?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I do try to tell myself that. Like, maybe it’s just the angle or the video, or maybe I’m seeing it worse than it is.\n\nBut then when I watch the lesson videos, I just can’t stop seeing that one side looking crooked and swollen. And once I’ve noticed it, it feels really hard to believe other people aren’t noticing it too — the students, parents, staff… I start thinking they must be staring at my face instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nI know that probably sounds… excessive. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your relationships with colleagues, friends, or family members?\n\nPatient: A bit, yes… I mean, I’ve tried not to let it affect anyone else, but it has affected work more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on lesson planning and mentoring things, and even basketball coaching stuff, because I keep not getting things finished on time. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job, but… administrators have started documenting missed deadlines and coverage problems.\n\nI’m honestly worried I might be getting close to losing my position. That feels embarrassing to say.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face mainly about this one specific issue of it looking crooked or swollen, or are there other aspects of your appearance that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that one thing, I think… the one side of my face looking crooked or sort of swollen in the lesson videos.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but once I see it, I can’t really unsee it. Then I start feeling like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying. That’s the part I keep getting stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face caused you to avoid any social or occupational activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, mostly with work. I don’t want to overstate it, but I’ve fallen behind on lesson planning and mentoring, and even the basketball coaching things, because I just don’t get everything finished on time anymore.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where administrators have started documenting missed deadlines and coverage problems. I’m trying to fix it, I really am, but… I’m worried I might be close to losing my position. That’s probably the biggest way it’s affected me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your face if you were certain that no one else noticed or cared about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, because that would make more sense, wouldn’t it? But… honestly, I think I’d still see it.\n\nThe main thing is that I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen in the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s like it takes over, and then I start thinking the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo even if you told me nobody cared… I think I’d still be stuck on it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face is more about the way it actually looks or about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but it starts with the way it looks to me.\n\nI’m here because I can’t stop seeing that one side of my face looking crooked or swollen in the lesson videos. And then once I notice it, I sort of jump to thinking the students, parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your face if you never had to appear in front of students, parents, or staff again?\n\nPatient: Maybe… a little? I mean, I’d probably feel less exposed if I didn’t have to be in front of students or parents or staff.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely go away. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, my mind just goes straight to, “They’re all staring at that instead of listening to me.”\n\nI know that probably sounds… dramatic, but that’s what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face would decrease if you were assured that there was nothing physically wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It might help a bit, I suppose… like, I’d want to believe you if you said there was nothing physically wrong.\n\nBut I think I’d still keep seeing it in the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to let go of, and then I start thinking the students, parents, and staff must be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\nI know that probably doesn’t sound very rational. I’m not trying to be difficult — it just feels very convincing when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face would be the same if you were the only person who ever saw the lesson videos?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… less intense, maybe, if I was truly the only one who ever saw them.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be gone. I’d still notice that crooked or swollen-looking side of my face in the videos, and I’d probably still get stuck on it. The part with students, parents, and staff makes it worse, because then I start thinking they must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself — that’s just the loop I seem to get into.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your face decrease if you were certain that others perceived it as normal, even if you still saw it as flawed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would probably decrease some, if I could really be certain they saw it as normal.\n\nI mean, that’s the part that feels so awful — on the lesson videos I notice this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I just assume the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo if I knew they weren’t seeing it that way, I’d like to think I’d feel calmer. But… I’m not sure it would completely stop, because I’d still be seeing it myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the flaw you see in your face isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose there’s some possibility. I mean, I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut when I see myself on the lesson videos, it really looks like one side of my face is crooked or swollen, and once I notice it I can’t stop seeing it. Then I start thinking the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nI know you might tell me it isn’t there, and I do want to consider that. It’s just… in the moment, it feels very real to me. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m trying to fix it, I really am, but… I’m worried I might be close to losing my position. That’s probably the biggest way it’s affected me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your face if you were certain that no one else noticed or cared about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, because that would make more sense, wouldn’t it? But… honestly, I think I’d still see it.\n\nThe main thing is that I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen in the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s like it takes over, and then I start thinking the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo even if you told me nobody cared… I think I’d still be stuck on it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face is more about the way it actually looks or about what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but it starts with the way it looks to me.\n\nI’m here because I can’t stop seeing that one side of my face looking crooked or swollen in the lesson videos. And then once I notice it, I sort of jump to thinking the students, parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\n\nI know that may sound a bit excessive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less concerned about your face if you never had to appear in front of students, parents, or staff again?\n\nPatient: Maybe… a little? I mean, I’d probably feel less exposed if I didn’t have to be in front of students or parents or staff.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely go away. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, my mind just goes straight to, “They’re all staring at that instead of listening to me.”\n\nI know that probably sounds… dramatic, but that’s what keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face would decrease if you were assured that there was nothing physically wrong with it?\n\nPatient: It might help a bit, I suppose… like, I’d want to believe you if you said there was nothing physically wrong.\n\nBut I think I’d still keep seeing it in the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it’s really hard to let go of, and then I start thinking the students, parents, and staff must be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\nI know that probably doesn’t sound very rational. I’m not trying to be difficult — it just feels very convincing when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your face would be the same if you were the only person who ever saw the lesson videos?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… less intense, maybe, if I was truly the only one who ever saw them.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be gone. I’d still notice that crooked or swollen-looking side of my face in the videos, and I’d probably still get stuck on it. The part with students, parents, and staff makes it worse, because then I start thinking they must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m repeating myself — that’s just the loop I seem to get into.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your face decrease if you were certain that others perceived it as normal, even if you still saw it as flawed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would probably decrease some, if I could really be certain they saw it as normal.\n\nI mean, that’s the part that feels so awful — on the lesson videos I notice this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I just assume the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nSo if I knew they weren’t seeing it that way, I’d like to think I’d feel calmer. But… I’m not sure it would completely stop, because I’d still be seeing it myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the flaw you see in your face isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose there’s some possibility. I mean, I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut when I see myself on the lesson videos, it really looks like one side of my face is crooked or swollen, and once I notice it I can’t stop seeing it. Then I start thinking the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nI know you might tell me it isn’t there, and I do want to consider that. It’s just… in the moment, it feels very real to me. Does that answer what you mean?"
